1981. CHARLES RUSSELL was indicted , for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously wounding 1 mare, value 30 l., the property of Robert Bond.
ROBERT BOND . I live at West End, Hampstead. The prisoner was in my employ—on the 3rd of August, I lent him a cart to get manure—there was a mare in it—it was in a perfect state when he went away-when he brought her back she had a very deep wound under her throat, cut with a sharp instrument—it appeared a stab—I asked him how he came to think of stabbing the mare—he said, "Lord bless you, Sir, I would not do any thing of the sort to the dear animal"—I took it out of his charge and gave it to another man—I told him he must answer for his conduct—I sent my son for an officer, and while he was gone the prisoner made his escape.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. I believe the mare was able to work next day? A. It has worked since—I think the wound would have killed it if it had not glanced aside—it was about an inch and a half deep, and about as long—it might very likely be done with such a knife as labourers use to cut their victuals with—he had brought one load home when I saw the mare—I had not seen him for an hour and a half.
WILLIAM LIVOCK . I live in East-street, Hampstead. On the 3rd of August, I saw the prisoner stab the mare with a knife—I crossed the road directly—I saw him hold the wound with his fingers, he said, "I am sorry for what I have done"—he was very drunk, but it seemed to sober him after he had done it.
NOT GUILTY .

1982. SARAH MILLER was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Walker, on the 22nd of August, and stealing therein, 1 sheet, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 towel, value 6 d.; and 1 table-cloth, value 1 s.; the goods of Maria Walker: and 1 pair of trowsers, value 4 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1 s.; 1 shirt, value 2 s. 6 d.; and 2 handkerchiefs, value 4 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Young.
MARIA WALKER . I am a widow, and live with my son, Thomas Walker, in Crown-court, Shadwell. On Tuesday morning, the 2nd of August, between eight and nine o'clock, I went out—I bolted the back door with two bolts, locked the front door, and left the key at a neighbour's-all my things were safe then—I left nobody in the house—I returned on Thursday evening, and found the house in confusion—the back door and every thing was fast—I missed things from my bed-room—I cannot tell how any one had got in—I found nothing broken—I missed all the property stated.
THOMAS WALKER . I live with my mother. On Thursday morning, at a quarter before six o'clock, I left home-John Young, who lodges in. the house, had occasion to go backwards, and I told him to fasten the doors, but I did not see him do it—I returned at a quarter past twelve o'clock, and found the back door unbolted.
JOHN YOUNG . I bolted the door when I came out of the yard, and left it ten minutes before six o'clock—I came back in the evening, and found Mrs. Walker at home—the bolt I left fast anybody could open with a knife.
HENRY POTTER . I am shopman to Mr. Vaux, a pawnbroker, at Ratcliffe. I have a shirt which was pawned by the prisoner, on the 2nd of August, in the name of Ann Miller.
Prisoner. Q. You have known me some time? A. Yes—we always wrote her ticket "Ann."
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the shirt for 1 s. 9 d., on the Wednesday,
in Rosemary-lane, with some other things—there is no mark on it the prosecutrix can point out, but she told the Magistrate she knew it by her work—she told the neighbours that she suspected the young man had brought in an improper character to sleep with—I live next door to her.
MARIA WALKER . re-examined. I made the shirt myself, and know my own needle work—there is a mark in the middle of it—I told the pawn broker so before I saw it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380820-1983]
1983. WILLIAM HILLIAR was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, at Fulham, 2 sheets, value 1 l.; 1 cloak, value 12 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 12 s.; 1 pair of breeches, value 12 s.; 1 coat, value 1 l. 10 s.; and 1 jacket, value 1 l. 14 s.; the goods of Edward Baxter, in his dwelling house.
EDWARD BAXTER . I am a cowkeeper, and keep a house at Northend, Fulham. On the 19th of May a lad, named Shorter, who was in my employ, left me, without any notice—I had before that the property stated in the indictment in my box—I left home that morning, with ray milk, about seven o'clock-Shorter had the key of the front gate, and the key of that gate fitted the stable—he had the key of the house—I returned about nine o'clock, and found my box broken open, and my things gone, and Shorter also—the house door was left open, and the gate locked—( looking at some things)—these are part of the things I lost—the value of them all was 6 l. nearly—I am sure it was above 5 l.—I know the prisoner—I had seen him the day before, the 18th, with Shorter, but never again till he was at the station-house, three or four months afterwards—I knew him as having seen him with Shorter on the 18th in the field.
Cross-examined by MR. KEENE. Q. Was he dressed the same at the station-house? A. No—he had brown clothes on, and the day before the robbery he had a cap and a brown long coat—he was with my boy, and I asked him what he did there—he said he had no work to do, and had no victuals, and was in great distress—I carried my boy a good breakfast, and he gave him part of it—Shorter is a short, thick, old-fashioned looking fellow, quite a lost lad, and he has rather a scabby face—I can identify all the property here—it is not all I lost—I should value this at about 30 s.
SARAH BROWN . I am the wife of John Brown, and live at Northend, Fulham. On Saturday morning, the 19th of May, about half-past eight o'clock, as I was returning from my husband's breakfast, the prisoner came out of the alley, with a bundle under his arm—it looked like clothes pinned up—he turned round, whistled down the alley, and Shorter followed after him, with another bundle under his arm—they both joined together, and went towards Hammersmith.
DANIEL BAXTER FOLKARD . I am assistant to my father, who is a pawn-broker, at Brentford—I produce a pair of breeches and a pair of trowsers which were pawned on the 19th of May, for 3 s.—I have not a doubt it was the prisoner, but I will not swear to him—they were pawned in the name of Edward Baxter, lodger, by his son.
Cross-examined. Q. You are not certain it was the prisoner? A. I have not a doubt of it—I could not swear to him—I told the policeman I recollected a little spot on his face—I do not see that now.
JOHN RADFORD . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—I asked Folkard if he knew the prisoner—he told me he had no doubt it was him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say he recognised him in consequence of his having a mark on his face? A. No; to the best of my knowledge he did not—he said he was marked with the small-pox—that is all I recollect—he said he knew his features by that.
COURT. Q. Did you find any money on him? A. 8 l. 18 s. 1 1/2 d.
GUILTY of stealing to the value of 80 s. only.* Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18380820-1984]
1984. RICHARD WOODWARD was indicted for a robbery on Eliza Greening, on the 7th of August, putting her in fear, and taking from her person and against her will, 1 umbrella, value 3 s., the goods of Henry Greening.
ELIZA GREENING . I am the wife of Henry Greening, who is a writer, and live in William-street, James-street, Westminster-about half-past eight o'clock on the night of the 7th of August I was in Birdcage-walk, and met the prisoner—he used indecent language—I told him he was mistaken in the person, and told him to go on—he still accosted me, and I turned round to go back to his guard-room to complain of him, when he snatched my umbrella from my hand, and ran off—he took it by force—I could not resist, he took it so suddenly—I told a young man who stood by to run after him, which he did, and called "Stop thief!" and the policeman took him—this is my umbrella—( looking at it.)
Prisoner. Q. Did not you say I should be put into the guard-room if you complained? A., Yes; I said I did not wish to do any thing of the kind, but you still insulted me, and almost pulled my shawl off my back-you did not tell me when I was going towards the baracks that I should have any umbrella again-you ran off with it directly you took it.
JOSEPH BIRCH . I am a tailor, and live in Leicester-place. I was in Birdcage-walk, and saw a soldier and a female standing there—I had not gone many yards before the female called out that the soldier had taken her umbrella, and was going away with it, and asked me to go and get it from him—he was walking away with it—I called to him, and he commenced running—I called "Stop thief"—the policeman asked what was the matter—I said he had taken an umbrella, and he pursued him—he was little in liquor I think—it was the prisoner.
BASIL MIDDLETON . I am an officer. I heard the cry of "Stop thief!" and went up to Birch, who told me that the soldier had run away with the umbrella—I went after him, and overtook him in Cartwright-street—I asked him who the umbrella belonged to—he put his back against the rails, and tried to drop it down the railings—I took it up, and took him back to the prosecutrix—I asked if it belonged to her, and she said "Yet"—he said he had taken it for a lark.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in liquor at the time it happened, but as far as I can recollect, I met the female-what I said to her I cannot say—she said she would go to the barracks and report me—I think I took it out of her hand to prevent her going to report me.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.

JOHN VIVIAN was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John ThompSon, about the hour of ten in the night of the llth of August, at St. Luke, with inttet to steal, and stealing therein, 18 spoons, value 9 l. 10 s.; 16 forks, value 10 l.; 6 knives, value 2 l. 10 s.; 3 ladles, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 fish-slice, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 coffee-pot, value 7 s.; and two wine-strainers, value 5 s.; the goods of the said John Thompson.
MR. PAYNE. conducted the Prosecution.
MARY CORNER . I am the wife of John Corner, and was in the service of Mr. John Thompson, of Albion-terrace, King's-road, Chelsea—the prisoner is my brother, and was in the habit of coming to see me about once a week or fortnight, at master's house—I saw him there on the Tuesday before the examination—on the Saturday night before I went before the Magistrate on Monday, I left the house between nine and ten o'clock—I will not be positive it was after nine o'clock—it was after eight o'clock—I had the care of the house—Mr. Thompson was out of town—I left no body in the house when I went out—I pulled the door after me, but I will not be positive whether it was shut—I came back before ten o'clock, and found the door shut—I opened it with a key, and went in, and missed the property stated in the indictment—it had been wrapped up is a leather-when I went out it was safe on a large table—I should think it worth about 20 l.—I spoke to the policeman directly, and he came in and examined the house—my mother had been with me that day—on the Sunday morning, between nine and ten o'clock, I went with my mother over to Tooting to my brother's lodging—he asked us why we came there a early in the morning—my mother said we had lost some plate—he said he had not seen it—he at last took us to Wandsworth-common, kicked a turf with his feet, and there was the plate, wrapped up in the leather as before—we all three came along the road together, and met a policeman—my brother then turned away from us, and was going away from the common—the policeman went after him, and brought him back—he then came after my mother and me, and took us all to the station-house—I think the plate is master's—it is the same leather and the same plate that was in the box.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of larceny only. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy. Confined Six Months.

1986. JULIA KEATING was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, at St. Mary, Islington, 7 sovereigns, the monies of Lydia Catherine Lemoyne, her mistress, in her dwelling-house.
LYDIA CATHERINE. LEMOYNE . I am single, and live in Canonbury-lane, in the parish of St. Mary, Islington. The prisoner was my cook I left home on the 12th of August, and went to church—I returned in the middle of the day—I went to church again in the evening, and when I returned, found the prisoner intoxicated in the fore-court—she was brought in and put to bed, and after counting my money in my box, I found seven sovereigns had been taken away—the box was still locked—I believe I had left my keys in my room, but I am not exactly sure—I sent for a constable—we went into the prisoner's room, and found six sovereigns and nine shillings and sixpence in her pocket—she was taken to the station-house—I found my keys hanging in a drawer in a chest in my own room.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Had not you left them there? A. I do not know—I might possibly have done so—I had seen them last before I went to church in the morning—I found them in my drawer after the morning service—the housemaid was at church in the morning—my sister lives with me—we keep a school—we share the profits of the school,
but the seven sovereigns were my own, and not connected with the school—I had counted my money on Saturday night, the last thing before I went to bed—it was in a common wooden box—the money taken was my own—there were forty-two sovereigns in the box; eighteen were in a purse, and there were six papers, each containing four sovereigns-five of the papers had been opened, and one sovereign extracted from each—it was not joint property—the prisoner was insensibly drunk.
ANN BECKWITH . I am the prosecutrix's servant. I was at home on this Sunday—I found the prisoner drunk, and took her to bed-her pocket dropped—I took it up, and the policeman was sent for.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you at home all the evening during your mistress's absence? A. I was at church in the morning—I was at home in the evening—the prisoner went out to go to church, and I saw no more of her till mistress came home, and found her drunk in the fore-court.
JOHN GIBLETT . I am a policeman. I was sent for, and found the prisoner lying on the bed, drunk—I found a letter in her pocket—after she dressed herself, I took her pocket from under her pillow, and found six sovereigns, nine shillings and sixpence in silver, and sixpence-half-penny in copper, in it.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Transported for Ten Years.
NEW COURT.— Monday, August 27 th, 1838.

1990. WILLIAM FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of August, 1 adze, value 2 s., the goods of the London Dock Company.
RICHARD NANCARROW . I am an officer of the Customs. I stopped the prisoner on the 10th of August, at West-garden-gate, London Docks, and found on him a cooper's adze—he said he had taken it from one of
the cooper's mates, and had knocked the handle out to grind the adze—that he had put it in his pocket in a lark, and did not know it was there.
GEORGE DIX . I am a constable in the London Docks. I received the prisoner in charge. I asked him where he got the adze—he said, on the quay—I think he said the East quay—I went there, and Sanders had lost his adze—it is marked with the London Dock Company's mark.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you known the prisoner working in the dock? A. No, I might have seen him working there.
THOMAS SANDERS . I am a cooper in the employ of Mr. Haywood—he contracts for the London Dock Company's coopering. I left my tools on the head of a puncheon in the Docks—I missed this adze.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know this man before? A. No, he was a stranger in the Dock.
WILLIAM ALLARS . I know this adze, it is one of a dozen which belonged to the London Dock Company
HENRY ASHCROFT . I am vault-keeper of the rum vault, East Vaults, London Docks. The prisoner worked on the quay at our vault, but has left about three months, with a good character.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380820-1991]
1991. SAMUEL CRAWCOUR was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 4th of August, of an evil-disposed person, 2lbs. weight of tea, value 10 s.; and 4th of weight of tortoiseshell, value 10 s.; the goods of the London Dock Company.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS. and BALLANTINE. conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES ROBERTSON DUNKINSON . I am a Customs gate-keeper at the London Dock. I was on duty at the west entrance, on the afternoon of Saturday, the 4th of August—I saw the prisoner coming out, and in consequence of something I observed, I stopped him about half-past four o'clock—I asked him what he had in his hat—he said, "A little tea"—I then asked what he had in his pockets—he said, "A little tea"—I searched him, and found in his hat about 1 1/2 lbs. weight of tea, and in his pockets two packets of tea, about 1/4 lb.—they were wrapped up in whitey-brown paper—I then asked what he had got in his stomach—he said, "Apiece of tortoiseshell, "and he gave me this piece of tortoiseshell from between his shirt and trowsers—I asked where he got the tea and tortoiseshell—he said the mate of a ship, from China, gave them him—I asked if he could show us the ship—he said, "Yes, and it appeared to have tea in the hold"—I went with him, and when we got to the East quay, he said, "It is no use taking you any farther; to tell you the truth, one Mr. Avery gave it me".
COURT. Q. Did he speak of having all the goods from Avery? A. I understood he had them both from one or the other party.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Does tea come from China? A. Yes, and I believe tortoiseshell does—the prisoner is the son of a respectable man, I have heard-his father was at the office—it is an almost every-day occurrence for mates of ships to make up samples, and get persons to smuggle them out—these small parcels of tea look like samples but the parcel in the hat was so large it must have been seen by any person-tortoiseshell pays a duty, I believe.
JOHN CLEMENTS . I am a constable. I went with the prisoner and Mr. Dunkinson, round the Docks, for the prisoner to point out the ship from which he got the tea-when we got to the East quay, he said, "It is no
use my taking you about in this way, I will tell you the truth, Avery gave me the tea and the tortoiseshell"-Avery is a manager of teas at No. 2 ware house, London Docks—he has no right to give any tea away—I took the prisoner to the watch-house, and then went and found Avery—I took him to the watch-house, and said to the prisoner, "Is that the man that gave you the tea and tortoiseshell?"—the prisoner said, "Yes"-Avery said with surprise, "I give you the tea!"—the prisoner remained silent-A very said, "It is false, by G-."
FIANCIS HILL . I am an officer of the Customs at the tea warehouse. I saw the prisoner on the 4th of August, in the tea-warehouse—he asked A. No he was for Mr. Avery—I told him he was in the building, and if he would go on, he would find him—the tea produced is worth about 10 s.—the duty is 2 s. 11 d. per pound—the teas in bond are the property of the merchants—they are in the custody of the London Dock Company.
JOHN TREEMER . I am in the employ of the London Dock Company. I saw the prisoner in the warehouse No. 2, on Saturday, the 4th of August—I know Avery—I had left him a few minutes before in the ware-house, on business—I saw the prisoner in Avery's room—I believe he was acquainted with him—I had seen him in company with him before that day—this paper which is round the tea, found on the prisoner, is similar to what is used in the Dock warehouse for papering green tea—we had chests of tea opened in No. 2 warehouse the day before for tareing—the officers of the establishment had access to that warehouse while the tea was tareing, and so had Avery—he was superintendent of that ware-house—I cannot say how many chests of tea were opened, but of the description of tea found on the prisoner, we might have perhaps twenty opened on the Thursday and the Friday—this is precisely of the same description as that in the chests-when the tea is tared, the chests are entirely emptied—the tea is weighed and put in again, and the chests are nailed down-Avery was present—there were no chests of this particular description of tea opened on Saturday the 4th of August—the value of this tea found on the prisoner is about 10 s., including the duty, which is 2 s. 1 d. a pound—it is green tea.
Cross-examined. Q. Is not that a good price for it? A. It is what I think it would fetch in the trade—I heard the officer say, there was about 2lbs. of it—it is young hyson—there are two sorts of tea which are more common, twankay and hyson-skin—it is perhaps two months since we had any tea of this sort open before in that warehouse—I cannot tell what had been opened in the other warehouse—there is only one other warehouse that has tea in it.
JOHN COOK . I am a foreman in the London Docks. Some tortoise-shell was opened for public sale on the 25th and 27th of July—some part of it was left at the Docks—this piece found on the prisoner is of the same kind, but it ran of various shapes and sizes—the officers of the Company had access to the place where this tortoiseshell was kept-Avery came there sometimes, but very seldom-tea and tortoiseshell are sometimes left on board the ships, and not taken to the warehouses directly—after the sale, the tortoiseshell was packed into the cases, weighed, and put away-no person could have got at that tortoiseshell in any private manner on the 4th of August—they must have broken a chest open of considerable size.
EDWARD WILLIAM SIMMONS . I was present on the first examination
of the prisoner, at the Thames police-office—he made a statement, on the 4th of August, which I took down, and it was signed by the Magistrate—this is it—( looking at it)—( read)-'The prisoner says, 'I had them given to me by Avery, in the tea-warehouse, about ten minutes to three o'clock—he gave me the tortoiseshell as well as the tea—that is truth'"—on the 6th of August the prisoner was examined again, and he then said, in reply to an observation made by Avery—( reading)-"He did not give me the property, but I thought he had the power to let me pass at the gate—it was given me by a sailor, a mate of a ship—I don't know the ship I had it brought to me—I was sitting on a barrel, on the side of the Dock."
RICHARD AVERY . I am manager of the tea department, at No. 2 warehouse. I have had a knowledge of the prisoner the last five months.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you employed in the Docks at the present time? A. I am not.
COURT. Q. you. need not answer the question, you may use your own discretion. Did you give the prisoner the tea or the tortoiseshell produced? A. On my oath I did not—I had not the slightest knowledge whatever of his having it—I never gave any facility to him to take any thing out of the Dock—I am so surrounded that it would be impossible for me to do it—the prisoner might have called, on me—I know, as a matter of fact, that goods are smuggled through the Docks from the ships.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380820-1992]
1992. JOHN BILL was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 2 pints of brandy, value 5 s., and 1 pint of wine, value 2 s., the goods of the London Dock Company, his masters.
MR. BALLANTINE. conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE DIX . I am a policeman. In consequence of information I went with a man named Clements to the East Vault Office, London Docks, on the 14th of August—the prisoner was employed in that office as a clerk—I asked Clements to examine the prisoner's desk—it is a large desk which extends from one side of the box to the other—I should think it is about eight feet—there are others sit there besides him—there is a small cupboard under his desk from the ground up to the desk—the prisoner unlocked the door of the cupboard—I searched it all round, and I found a small bag, but nothing of consequence—I perceived that the inside of the cupboard did not extend so high as the outside-by pulling a drawer out in the desk, he could get to the top of this cupboard-while the drawer was closed I could not see any opening—I pulled the drawer out entirely, and saw a board which appeared to have been recently planed—I pulled up that board, put my hand in, and found three bottles containing two pints of brandy, and one pint of wine—I examined the cupboard well—there was no means of getting to the place where I found the bottles without opening the drawer—the desk does not open—it was by pulling the drawer out, I got to the top of the cupboard, and the prisoner unlocked the drawer—I asked whether he could give any account of these bottles—he said he knew nothing of them, nor yet of the place—he had been in the employment of the Docks a long time—he came there when quite a lad.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you the first officer employed, or was Mr. Hogg? A. I have no knowledge of any one but myself
and Clements—I had a letter from Mr. Edwards, but I have not got it present—I believe it is in the hands of the solicitor—I have seen a paper called the "Town"—I believe I have seen the number of the 18th of august—I do not doubt that I have—I see most of them.
Q. Look at this letter in it—( handing the paper to the witness)-did you sea that? A. Yes—I received the letter signed Edwards before I saw this paper or heard of it—I received the letter on Friday, the 10th of August—( looking at a letter)—this is the letter I received then—I know Mr. lames Edwards—he is here in Court—he is foreman to Lady Carman—I believe he has other names besides Edwards—I believe his names are Edwards Andrew Pearson—it was in consequence of this letter that I made my inquiries and my searches.
Q. Did you learn from James Edwards that the prisoner had written a letter to the "Town" about the clerks in the office, and that he would give him a turn? A. I did not—I know Betts—I cannot say whether he is an acquaintance of Pearson's—I know Groom—I cannot say whether he is an acquaintance of Betts's.
COURT. Q. You. went to search the prisoner's desk in consequence of' receiving that letter? A. Yes—I heard nothing of the "Town" till the Monday.
JOHN CLEMENTS . I was present at the finding of these bottels—the prisoner said he knew nothing about them, and did not know even of the existence of the place.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you searched him first on the Monday evening? A. Yes; and found nothing on him—I had searched the seat before that evening, but did not find the secret place.
ALEXANDER STEWART BETTS . I am an extra clerk in the East Vault Office, London Docks—I was present when Dix found the bottles of wine and brandy over a drawer in the prisoner's desk—the prisoner kept the key of the drawer—I have noticed that the prisoner has been in the habit of locking his drawer latterly when he went out and taking the key with him—the key was on a bunch of keys—I saw the bottles found—this is one of them—( looking at a bottle)—I have seen this bottle before at the office in the prisoner's possession—it was then empty—I saw it a fortnight or three weeks before, in the prisoner's cupboard I think.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he been in the London Dock? A. I cannot say—he has been in our place about ten months—I have known him in the Docks about two years—he and I were on very good terms as clerks sitting together—I could not help feeling sorry when the officers made this discovery—I was a good deal surprised—there have not been any irregularities in the Docks to my knowledge—the prisoner is rather given to writing lampoons, and it seems according to the "Town," that he has been writing them against his friends.
Q. Have you any means of knowing that he has written lampoons against his friends and against yourself, except from the "Town?" A. yes—I saw him writing a paper I think on, the Saturday week previous to its appearing in the "Town"-at least he was writing it on the Thursday and Friday, and I suppose it was sent on the Saturday—there was only the prisoner and I sat on that side of the desk, and on the other side Mr. Treble and Mr. Patrick sat.
Q. As you saw he was writing on the Thursday and Friday, did you suspect he was going to send a lampoon? A. No—we sat so close that it
was impossible for one to write without the other seeing him—I happened to catch a glimpse of the word "Town," and having seen the paper called the "Town," that arrested my attention.
Q. Did you crib the draft of it from under his portfolio? A. I did—he wrote it and kept a copy of it—that is the fact—it began as this letter does—Mr. Rundle is foreman of the vault, and I am casting clerk-when I cribbed this letter, I showed it to Mr. Mitchel, a superior officer, but to no one else—I have not had it since—I do not know Edwards—I know Pearson—he is a common man—I had some conversation with him about the letter-everybody in the Docks knew it—I do not know how soon after an officer was desired to be in attendance to see if he could find any thing on a gentleman wearing grey breeches—( looking at a letter.)I saw this at the police-office—I cannot say whether Pearson wrote it—it is disguised—I think some of the letters are very like his writing—it is a long time since I saw his writing.
Q. There is a secret bottom to this drawer or top to the cupboard, how long has it been there? A. I cannot say—I have known it there two years, ever since I have been there—that may be a nice place to put a bottle in, to get a man into a scrape, that writes in the Town —I do not know whether Pearson knew of that place, or whether the superior officer did—I never told them of it—I mentioned it at the Thames police, and to the constables—I do not know of any other secret place—I have not got one.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18380820-1993]
1993. JOHN M'CORMACK, WILLIAM SLOANE , and GEORGE SMITH , were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of July, 1 sheet, value 5 s.; 6 spoons, value 12 s.; 1 tea-pot, value 5 l., the goods of John Soy, in a vessel, called the Royal Adelaide, on the navigable river Thames.-2nd COUNT,. stating them to be the goods of Mary Coatsworth.
MESSRS. CLARKSON. and BALLANTINE. conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS LAWSON . I was a seaman on board the Royal Adelaide steam vessel. She was lying in the river, off St. Katharine's Docks—the there prisoners were employed as porters, in stowing the cargo—they were all three working in the hold with me—I was called up to get tea, at half-past five o'clock, and after that I sat down to smoke on the grating-Sloane came up with a sheet under his arm—I asked what he had got—he said some washed linen—I took it from him—it was a sheet marked "C. R."—I said there was something going on wrong—I went down into the hold, and saw M'Cormack with something, and asked what he had—he said he had nothing—I said he had something he ought not to have—he put it in his pocket—I took it out of his right-hand pocket, and it was half a dozen silver spoons—I called the mate and found a tea-pot lying by two cases, by M'Cormack's feet-Smith was on deck at that time, at his duty-Coyle and I examined the hold, and found a chest which had been broken—I did not observe any thing on the top of the chest—there appeared to have been things taken out—there was a space in the chest.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you given either of these men any thing to drink? A. No-no rum, nor wine, nor any sugar to eat-M'Cormack did not tell me he was going to give these spoons to the mate—he put them in his pocket, before my face—I cannot say how many. persons were on board-no one was in the hold but M'Cormack.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Sloane came up from the hold? A. Yes, and on taking the sheet from him, I went immediately down into the hold-john Soy is captain of that ship.
JOHN BARTLETT . I am a Thames police-constable. I was called on board the Adelaide, on Friday, the 6th of July, at half-past eight at night—these articles were delivered to me, and I took the three prisoners—I searched Smith on the deck, and found on him two ounces of tobacco—I asked M'Cormack where he got the spoons—he said he saw a piece of paper on one of the cases, and when he came to overhaul it, he found it to contain spoons, and put them into his pocket-Sloane said he picked up the sheet lying by the water-closet—he thought it was washed linen, and intended to give it to the steward.
THOMAS COYLE . I am shipping clerk to the agent of the Dublin and London Steam-packet Company. I was on board the Adelaide on the 6th of July—I received the chest—there was a ticket on it marked "No. 14," and in the evening it was discovered to be broken—the name of the captain is John Soy.
CHARLES HENRY FALCONER . I am a Thames police-surveyor. Bartlett reported to me that he had these men, and I sent for them—I told them I understood they were charged with breaking open a plate chest, on boarcl the Adelaide, but to be careful what they said-Sloane said, "I was going to the water-closet, and found the sheet, I kept it as was my duty, till I saw some of the crew, and when I came on deck I gave it to Lawson"-M'Cormack said, "I was going for a south-wester in another part of the hold, and on my way I found a parcel of spoons, I had them in my hand, but never put them into my pocket"-Smith gave an account of something found on him, but not of these things.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe that was a bit of tobacco? A. Yes.
SARAH CRAWLEY . I live in Bridge-street, Greenwich. Mrs. Coats-worth was my mistress—she lived at Mrs. Smallwood's, at Greenwich—she left for Falmouth on the 7th—I was with her till the Friday morning preceding—I corded up all her boxes but one large chest, which was too heavy for me—I saw it at her house before it went away—there was too direction on it to go to Mr. Coyle, to go on board the steam-packet—this sheet—( looking at it)-is hers—this tea-pot and spoons are hers—they were to go by the Royal Adelaide.
JOHN OSBORNE . lam manager of thiscompany—I went down on the morning of the 7th, as far as Blackwall—Mr. Farr brought a lady on board—I saw a chest on board—the lady saw it, and identified it as her property.
(M'Cormack and Sloane received good characters.)
M'CORMACK— GUILTY . Aged 26. SLOANE— GUILTY Aged 27.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined Six Months.
SMITH— NOT GUILTY .

1994. GEORGE SMITH was again indicted for stealing, on the 6th of july, 2oz. weight of tobacco, value 6 d., the goods of John Soy, in, the Royal Adelaide, on the navigable river Thames.
MESSRS. CLARKSON. and BALLANTINE. conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BARTLETT . I am a Thames police-constable. I was called on
board the Royal Adelaide, on the 6th of July—I searched Smith, and found on him two ounces of tobacco—he was given into my custody, but he escaped—I asked him where he got the tobacco—he said he took it out of a cask.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLITS. Q. Did he not tell you there was a cask with a piece broken out of the head, and that he put his hand in and took that bit, thinking it was no harm? A. Yes, he did—the price of this is about 6 d.
GEORGE WHITE . I am a Thames police-constable. I went in search of Smith—I took him into custody in the head sheets of a coal-barge-he said he had tried to get away from me, but he found it was of no use.
THOMAS COYLE . The name of the captain of the Royal Adelaide, is John Soy—I recollect receiving two hogsheads of tobacco on hoard, which were rather broken at the head.

[trialAccount t18380820-1995]
1995. HUMPHREY TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of August, 1 bag, value 2 d.; and 102 pairs of iron tips, value 5 s.; the goods of James Bold.
JAMES BOLD . I live in Parker-street, Drury-lane, and am a smith. On the 2nd of August I was with the prisoner in Long-lane—I had 112 pairs of tips in a bag—we had called at one shop, and he said he had sold one pair-when we came to Long-lane, we went into a public-house, and laid the bag on the table—the prisoner took it up, and ran away, and left me to pay for a pint of porter—I saw him again on the 17th.
Prisoner. I gave him an order for six or eight dozen pairs off 3/4 plates—I found him on the 2nd of August, in Drury-lane, with the work, and he gave me the bags. Witness. No, I deny it—he carried it sometime—he had given me no orders for the tips—I was going out with them to sell—I did not say, "You can look at them, and see if they suit your order"—I had not got them for him.
Prisoner. We went to a public-house, and I picked one dozen out that were suitable—I took 1 s., and we went and got dinner together, and some ale, out of the shilling. Witness. No, I deny it—he dragged my work off the table, and said to the landlord, "I will leave this man in pawn for ten minutes"-! did not authorise him to sell a shilling's-worth at Mr. Cuthbert's, on this order he is speaking about, he took the work and had eighteen shillings for them, and he gave me but eight shilings for them—that was another matter.
JOHN RADLEY . ( police-constable F 24.) I heard a cry of "Stop thief," at the corner of Queen-street, Seven Dials, on the 17th of August, and saw the prisoner running—I took him.
Prisoner. I had sold a shilling's-worth, and we went and drank, and ate together, and that night I sold eighteenpence worth more, at three-halfpence a pair—they were not worth going about with, and the Alderman ordered me to pay five shillings—I never was fetched to pay it, and them another Alderman ordered me to come here.
JAMES BOLD . re-examined. The tips were in a small leather bag, worth twopence, and the tips were worth 5 s.—he was not to sell them—I let him off as low as I could, that he might make the money up.
GUILTY . Aged 57.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18380820-1997]
1997. WILLIAM HENRY FRY was again indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 11 s., the goods of Edwin Bilton, his master.
MR. DOANE. conducted the Prosecution.
EDWIN BILTON . I am a boot and shoemaker, and live in Totten-ham-coart-road. The prisoner came into my service about sixteen months since—I missed a large quantity of property, and suspected him—I watched when he came from dinner, and communicated with Thornton, an officer-in the 17th of February I was in a room above looking through a hole in the ceiling-when he returned from dinner, between one and two o'clock, I observed him come to the corner of his board—he put his hat on the board and looked at the door—he crossed the shop to where I keep my boots and shoes, looked through the boots, and selected one boot—he had some difficulty to find the other—he found it in the window—he fetched two sheets of paper, and then keeping an eye to one door through which he thought I could see him, he rolled up one and put it into his coat-pocket—he had a large frock-coat on—he then took the other, wrapped that up, tried it round with string, and put it into his pocket—he then patted his lockets, put on his apron, crossed the shop, and hung up his hat which he had before laid on the board—I then communicated with the officer—I same down and told the prisoner to go to Soho-square—he left the shop, and I afterwards saw him in custody—these are my boots—( looking at them)—they were made for a customer, who, trying to get them on, burst own the back of one of them.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. you had him taken to Hatton garden? A. Of course—the Magistrate' differed in opinion with-before I had half gone through my case, he dismissed the charge—I him again apprehended at the office door—he was running away, and officer took him—the officer said, "You have three charges; this only one; you can take him again"—we then went to Bow-street—Mr. Twyford was the Magistrate there—he did not hear all I had to say—he said, "I cannot hear all a brother Magistrate has done; etiquette compels me to discharge him"—he said that there were three charges, and he wondered how Mr. Bennett could discharge him.
Q. Did you not admit that the prisoner had on other occasions taken boots and shoes from your shop without your knowing any thing about it, and had settled with you for them on Saturday night? A. No; I have served him twice as a customer—I stated that he had asked me for a pair of shoes for his sister, and another pair, soiled, for a servant, which I sold. him very cheap—I did not state that he took them out of the stock with-. out my knowledge, or without asking me—he said, "Can you let me have a soiled pair of shoes?" and I said, "Here are some soiled ones you can have at half-a-crown"—I said he had shoes out of my shop, and settled with me on Saturday night; and Mr. Bennett said, "He is not an honest man; he ought to have told you of these"—the prisoner had no customers of his own, to my knowledge—I had spoken to a policeman after I saw-him take first pair, before he took this pair—I had told the policeman
on Friday, and this occurred on the Saturday; but the policeman had been there the whole week—I had not had the prisoner taken up before-no one came to me for a reference—I did not know that he was engaged by a shoemaker at Norwich—he had mentioned nothing about learing my situation.
Prisoner. You said you would divide customers with me, and you knew I was going to Norwich-you said you would serve me out for it—you know I had one pair of boots for three months when the lady was out of town. Witness. No; you had a pair of shoes for three months, and I said he must pay on the Saturday night, if he did not return them.
COURT. Q. Had you previously known of his having them? A. Yes I had given him leave—I did not know who they were for.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you never sworn that you never served him but twice, and then as a customer? A. No; I should say I did not say so—I said, "I let him have two pairs—I served him on two occasions—he told me he knew a friend who would be very glad to purchase them, as they were an exceedingly strong pair, and if I would allow him, he would take them to try—he kept them, saying the party was at Richmond, and he could not get them—he never had boots or shoes from me, except the three pairs I have mentioned, to my recollection.
Q. Has he not had goods as many as half a dozen times, and settled with you for them on Saturday night? A. He has not half a dozen time—I think I am quite safe in swearing it was not five times—it may have been four times—I may have been mistaken in one—I preferred the charge before the Grand Jury—the prisoner has not been committed by any Magistrate.
COURT. Q. When did you prefer your bill? A. On the 17th of April, I believe, while the Grand Jury were sitting—I have never had him apprehended.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What was the reason you did not take him? A. I could not find him—I waited outside his mother's house while the officer went in—I did not go to his mother's house for him after that—I told the policeman to keep a look-out—I heard he was driving a cab, and I kept looking at the cabs —it was after the second hearing that I went to Bow-street—the week before he was taken, he returned me the pair of ladies' shoes that did not fit the customer-his mother did not appear when he was before Mr. Bennett, and bring forward a pair of shoes, that I saw—there was no pair brought forward that had been part of my stock—I swear it was not so—she did not produce a pair of boots—I did not see her within sufficient distance to produce anything—she stated nothing there—I swear that without her being heard Mr. Bennett dismissed the prisoner—he was taken up ultimately on the charge of bigamy—he was remanded twice, I believe—I did not tell my story there before the Magistrate—I handed in the certificate of his being indicted.
MR. DOANE. Q. you had got your bill found by the Grand Jury? A. Yes; that was by the advice of Mr. Twyford-all the settlements I had with the prisoner alluded to the boots and shoes I had sold him—I never allowed him to take boots or shoes out without my consent.
GEORGE THORNTON . ( police-constable E 91.) In consequence of directions from the prosecutor, I was in attendance outside his shop, on the 17th of February—he made some communication to me—the prisoner went
up Chenis-street into Gower-street—I went up to him, and asked what he had got in his pocket—he said, "Halloo, what do you want?"—he said, "My own property"—I said, "Where did you bring them from?"—he said, "I am not going to tell you"—I took one boot from one pocket, and the other from the other—I said, "Tell me where you are going to take these"—he said, "I shall not, I shall tell your superiors."
Cross-examined. Q. You were at Hatton-garden? A., Yes; the prosecutor said that six months before, the prisoner had said, "Have you got a pair of soiled shoes for my mother's servant?" and he gave him five pairs—that he brought four pairs back, and paid him for one—he said he might have told him one more pair—Mr. Bennett said he thought there had been a bona fide transaction, and if he were on a Jury, he should have a doubt whether he did not take these boots for the purpose of selling them—the prosecutor's shop opens with folding doors—there is a blind goes before the cutting-board—the prosecutor had come to me on Friday evening, and requested me to witch—he said, very likely there would be something done then, as he had taken a pair the day before, and had not given him any account of them—I had been watching two or three days before—he requested me to come early on the Saturday, and follow the prisoner.
MR. PRENDERGAST. to EDWIN BILTON. Q. Was not the prisoner regularly employed in selling boots and shoes in your shop? A. Certainly; he was my clicker—he was employed to cut out leather, and if an extra hand was wanted, be went in to sell—he took money—I had employed him ten months.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Three Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18380820-1998]
1998. ANN DAVIES was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of July, 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 watch-ribbon, value 2 d.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; and 1 waistcoat, value 5 s.; the goods of Daniel Evans.
DANIEL EVANS . I am a servant, and live with Benjamin Hall. On the 30th of July I met the prisoner in Edgeware-road, about half-past eleven o'clock, or after—I was not quite sober—I entered into conversation with her—I cannot say whether she spoke to me first, or I to her—I took her to my room over the stable, in Old Church-street, Paddington—I gave her 2 s. 6 d.—we went to bed together, about twelve o'clock—I awoke about four o'clock the next morning, and she was gone—I looked on the table, and missed my watch—I went to the next room, and saw there was a shirt taken off the back of the chair, and this waistcoat—the watch was found—this is it—( looking at it).
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you in service? A. I was—I went by the name of Daniel Evans, but no other name—I swear that—I live at No. 22, Old Church-street, Paddington, with Mr. Benjamin Hall—I was not in that house by the name of David—I never answered to it—the prisoner asked me for gin—I told her she might have what she liked, and I would pay for it, and she had what she thought proper—I believe she called for half-a-pint, or sixpennyworth, or something of that sort, I do not know the measures—I do not know how much gin she drank, but what she called for, I paid for—we went to the Wheatsheaf, at the corner of old church-street—I gave her leave to call for what she liked—I cannot say whether I took any—I had taken a few pints of ale before-what I had
drank affected my head, of course, or else I should be able to answer about the drink-between myself and my friend we might drink four or five pints of ale-a very little would affect me-to the best of my knowledge I paid fivepence or sixpence for the prisoner—I do not believe that it was a shilling. I cannot swear it—lam sure she did not drink half-a-crown's worth of gia, because I had not so much in my pocket-when I went to the public-house I had about eightpence or ninepence—I was not sober when I went into the public-house—I was sober when I went to the first public-house—I there spent about eighteenpence—I had half-a-crown when I went there.
Q. Then you must have had a shilling when you left? A. Yes but I might have spent it elsewhere-if I see any thing in the street I may buy it—I cannot say whether I did buy any thing—the prisoner's sister has called on me—I sent a man to see for her, when I understood she wanted to see me—the man's name was Jones—I never went to her sister's residence—I found she was looking for me two or three times at a public-house, and I sent to see what she had got to say—I went to see her three or four hours afterwards—I never said I never went to see her.
ALFRED BLUNDELL ( police-constable E 24.) On Saturday, the 4th of August, I met the prisoner in Devonshire-street, Marylebone—that was some five days after this occurred—I told her I wanted her—she said, "For what?"—I said, "For robbing that man you went home with on Monday night"—she said, "It could not be me"—I said, "You are the person I saw in company with him"—she said, "I was not out on Monday night"—I took her to the station-house, and just before I got there she asked if she could see the man privately—I asked, "What man?"—she said the man she was with on Monday night—I said, What do you want to see him for?"—she made no answer—she again said, "Can I speak to him?" I said, "What do you want to speak to him for?"—she said, "I know nothing about any thing but the watch"—I said, "Don't you know about the other things?"—she said, "No"—I said, "If you know about the watch, you must know about the other things"—she then said that she had got the shirt at home, and the waistcoat her husband had got—I went to her room with her, and she gave me the duplicate of the watch, which was sewn up in a window-curtain—I have the shirt and waistcoat.
CHARLES FOWLER . I am journeyman to a pawnbroker. I have the watch—I cannot swear it was the prisoner who pawned it.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18380820-2000]
2000. ANN CONLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of August, 3 sovereigns, 5 shillings, and 3 sixpences, the monies of Thomas Jackson, from his person.
THOMAS JACKSON . I have been in the 80th regiment of foot eight years—I left the regiment in New South Wales, but was discharged at Chatham—on the 10th of August I was in London, and fell in with the prisoner, about six o'clock in the afternoon, in Covent Garden—she was walking up the street—I was never in London before, and I asked her if she could tell me where I could get decent lodgings—she said she could get me lodgings—I told her if it was a decent house I would be obliged to her to show me, and I would pay her for her trouble—I treated her with a pot of beer, and gave her a small portion of gin—we then went to a house—we had a few words, and I gave her a crown for herself, and two shillings I paid for the room—we went up stairs, and went to bed—I folded up my trowsers, and placed them under the bolster—I had three sovereigns, five shillings, and three sixpences, which I had placed in my fob-pocket—I went to bed about a quarter before eight o'clock—I Was a little fatigued with travelling—I was just feeling drowsy, when I felt something raise my head—I might have been in bed three-quarters of an hour—I got up and felt, and my trowsers were not where I had placed them I found them on the floor—I felt in the fob, and the money was gone—I accused her of taking it—she was in the room, on the floor, not in bed—I had a few words with her, and she said she had not seen any money of mine—I called out for a policeman to be brought in—the police-man came, and I gave her in charge—there was one sovereign and two sixpences found, and one penny.
SARAH WHITE . I am servant at the house in question. The prosecutor came with the prisoner—I knew her before, but the man was a stranger—he came about half-past six o'clock, and paid me two shillings for the first-floor room—the prisoner came down in about a quarter of an hour with a sovereign, and asked me to get something to drink—I told her it was not allowed in the house—she went up stairs again—she had her stays, shift, and petticoat on—I cannot say whether she had her shoes on.
JOHN KNIGHT . ( police-constable F 21.) About nine o'clock, on the 10th of August, I was called to No. 3, Legg-alley, to take in charge the prisoner for robbing the prosecutor—I went up stairs, and found them both undressed—the prisoner found a sovereign, and gave it to me—I found a sixpence and a penny in the room—the prosecutor had had a little drink, but was not tipsy.
Prisoner's Defence. I met this man in Seven Dials, very drunk, with another soldier—he had a bundle, I said, "You will lose the bundle if you don't take care"—it dropped, and I took it up—he said, "You take care of it, I will reward you for it"—he went into one public-house—they would not serve him, he was so tipsy—he went into another, and had a Pot of beer, and a glass of gin for me—he then went to another, and had mother pot of beer, and a quartern of gin—he pulled out a sovereign and some silver, and the other man said, "Take care of your little money, and get yourself a comrade"—he then said, "Come, I will show you where I "lodge"—they went out for about ten minutes, leaving me and another woman, and the bundle—the prosecutor came back—he asked me to get him a lodging—I took him up Long Acre—he went to a house, and paid two shillings for a bed—I went up stairs and took the bundle, and when we went
up, having the drop of gin, I suppose, I did not know what I was doing, no more than he did—it was then about eight o'clock-about nine o'clock I insisted upon going, finding where I was, and what a disgrace I had brought on myself, to pick up with such a low wretch—he got up, and said I should not go home—he took my pocket that was on the floor, and said, "Now you may go"—I said, "You sha'n't have my money, there's a half-crown and threepence-halfpenny in it—I insisted upon having the pocket—he got out of bed, and struck me, and gave me two black eyes—he struck me more than a dozen times—I hallooed murder—the servant came up, and he banged the door in her face, and said he would bung her eye—the housekeeper then came up, and said, "I can't have this noise, I must bare the police up stairs"-two policemen came up, and the prosecutor gave me is charge-first he said he had lost a sovereign, and then he said he had lost three sovereigns-when I said I wished to go, he dropped some money in the bed, the jingle of it was no more than a sovereign and a shilling-when the policemen searched, the bed-clothes were taken up, the sovereign and one sixpence were found in the mattress—I took up the sovereign, and said, "Here is a sovereign," and then the policemen took me to Bow-street-while the prosecutor was striking me, he dropped a sixpence, which was picked up at the water-stand, but he had given me two sixpences for myself—he did not give me five shillings-when I was searched, I had nothing about me but the two sixpences—the officer was sent back to the house to fetch the woman, and she had one pound, eight shillings, or one pound, eighteen shillings, in her pocket—the prosecutor said he had returned from transportation.
COURT. to THOMAS JACKSON. Did you tell her you had returned from transportation? A. No, I lost my arm in the service, and got discharged with a pension.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Transported for Ten Years.

2001. THOMAS ELDRIDGE and GEORGE THAKES were indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of August, 1 jacket, value 5 s., the goods of Edmund Frederick Wright, in a certain vessel, in a certain port of entry and discharge.
HENRY MOORE . I am a labourer in the West India Docks. On the 23rd of August, I was at work in No. 1. warehouse—I saw the two prisoners coming round the corner, and stopped between two piles of wood, and I saw Thakes give Eldridge a jacket—he put it on-Thakes then said, "That will do, come on"—I met Mr. Stevenson, and told him.
ROBERT STEVENSON . I am a wharfinger in the Docks. Moore pointed out the two prisoners as they were coming towards the gate which leads out-Eldridge had the jacket on his back—they separated at the gate of the Export dock, then Eldridge took off the jacket, came towards me with it on his arm, and I took him.
JAMES COX . The two prisoners were given into my custody—I asked Eldridge if the jacket belonged to him—he said no, it was given to him by a man, but he did not know his name—I asked Thakes if he knew any thing of it—he said it was his, that he brought it to the Docks with him that morning.
EDMUND FREDERICK WRIGHT . I work on board a ship in the Docks. I left this jacket, which is mine, in the forecastle—I do not know the prisoners.
Eldridges Defence. I was unfortunately drawn into this—I have worked there fifteen years-in coming up, I heard a voice call "Halloo"—I
turned and saw this prisoner—he said, "Are you going to the Import Dock?"—I said "yes"—he said, "will you take this jacket up?"—It said—I "It is cold, I will put it on"—I went on to the Export-gate and stood there in the presence of the constable-finding the jacket was wet, I put it off, and stood there with it on my arm—the constable asked how I got the jacket—I told what I knew of it, that a man gave it me.
Thakes's Defence. If I had been on board the ship and stolen it, it is likely I should give it to this man to go to the gate where the officers—I was walking in the Docks, and saw a man who asked me to buy—I laid, "Did you come by it honestly?" he said, "Yes"—I said I did not want it—I gave him 6 d. for it.
(Eldridge received a good character.)
ELDRIDGE— GUILTY . Aged 45.
THAKES— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Confined Six Weeks.

CHARLES HARMAN . I Live in Whitecross-street. 2006. MARY BROOKS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of August, 1 pewter pot, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Smith.

About six o'clock, on the 17th of August, the prisoner came and called for half a pint of beer—I served her with it in a pint pot—she sat by the side of the bar, and there was a quart pot that some one had left-when I turned she and the pot were gone—I went and found her with the quart pot, and took her back—this is my master's pot.
JAMES MARTIN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner, and found the quart pot under her shawl, under her arm.
Prisoner. I had no shawl on—there was full half a pint of beer in the pot—I just took it outside the door to give to my daughter. Witness. there was a little beer in the bottom of the pot—she was one hundred and fifty yards from the house.
Prisoner. Some persons were drinking out of a quart pot—they went away and said, "Old lady, there is a little beer for you"—I emptied the half pint into the quart pot, and went to look for. my daughter outside—I was not two doors from the house.
JURY. to JAMES MARTIN. Q. Was the pot entirely covered with the Shawl? A. Yes.
GUILTY . Aged 47.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18380820-2009]
2009. MARY HOLLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, 4 spoons, value 20 s.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 3 s. 6 d.; 1 counter-pane, value 5 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 3 s. 6 d.; 2 pairs of boots, value 4 s.; I blanket, value 2 s.; 2 petticoats, value 2 s.; 1 towel, value 6 d.; and 1 napkin. value 3 d.; the goods of Ann Lydia Lane.
ANN LYDIA LANE . I am a widow, and live in Whitecross-place, Wilson-street. I get my living by hot-pressing—the prisoner has lived in the nighbourhood for some years—on the 16th of July she represented to me that she was in great distress, and asked permission to see a person who lay dead in my house-in passing from the parlour to the kitchen, she
said I had a great deal to do—I said I had—she said she should be happy to assist me—I said I did not want a person—she said she should be glad to do any thing; that her husband had turned her out, and she wanted a meal—I thought it very hard, and I allowed her to come to my house, and gave her food and employment—she continued there till the 18th—I then missed a spoon, and then several other articles; but I did not suppose them lost, because she was every meal thanking me for my kindness—I thought, having a young family of five orphan children depending upon me for support, that the things might be mislaid—I missed a great many articles, and on the 9th of August, I ascertained that the prisoner had taken some things—on the 10th I asked her about it—she denied it, and wished the Lord might strike her dead if she had robbed me—I said, "If you have ventured your body, do not venture your soul"—she said, there were other persons in the house—I said, "Yes; but they have been in the house a long time"—I then told her I had proved her to be a thief—after a great deal of talk she gave me some duplicates-while I was talking to her she absconded through the next house without a bonnet.
ANN COLLINGHAM . I searched the prisoner in Featherstone-street station-house—on the 10th of August I found fourteen duplicates upon her—they relate to this property.
JAMES FRANCIS THOMPSON . I am a pawnbroker. I have ten pledges which I took in of the prisoner mostly—I have a spoon, a petticoat, some holland, a towel, an apron, a pair of boots, a napkin, a handkerchief, another petticoat, a pair of sugar-tongs, salt-spoon, dessert-spoon, and table-spoon—they are not all my taking in-but I took in several of then from the prisoner.
GEORGE ATTENBOROUGH . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Crown-street. I have a pair of shoes, a blanket, and counterpane—I cannot say I took them from the prisoner; but these duplicates correspond with the articles.
SUSANNAH LANE . I live with the prosecutrix, who is my mother.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not come into the passage when the spoon was pawned, and ask you where your mother was, and you said very likely she was gone to Bow-street; and I said, "Your mother gave me a spoon to pledge?" A. No.
WILLIAM BRILL . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner, and twelve duplicates were found upon her.
ANN LYDIA LANE . re-examined. All this is my property—it is part of what I lost.
Prisoner. Q. On your oath, was not the first thing you gave me to pledge that silver spoon-you asked Mrs. Batty to pay you your rent—she said she must make the money to pay you, and then you gave me the spoon to pledge? A. No, I did not.
Prisoner. When I came in with the money for the spoon she was gone out, and I asked the little girl which way she went—I gave her the silver and the halfpence in the market. Witness. I never gave her any thing to pawn, and never had the money.
Prisoner. She gave me the halfpence, and brought home a leg of pork to dine with a tally-man that comes every other week to dine with her and then she went into the country-when she returned, she told me to get a leg of pork and some peas, and to make a currant pie—she left no money in the house—I said, "What am I to do?"—she said, "Do as boy can, as I took a counterpane, and got 5 s.—her little boy
and another went with me to Mr. Chappell's, in Fore-street, and she came home the next morning intoxicated—the sugar-tongs she gave me the morning she was going into the country with a pot-boy—they offered me 3 s. for them—I came back and said, "Mrs. Lane, Mr. Thompson won't give me but 3 s. "—she said, "That won't do to go so many miles with," she got the damask cloths, and I went to Mr. Attenborough's, and got 3 s. on each of them-and I went and bought half a pound of beef, a quarter of a pound of ham, and a quartern of shrub, and they took them with them—she came home next morning, and had breakfast and gin-and-cloves, and then she set off into the country again—then she saw a pair of old boots, and gave me the money to go and get them—she asked me for the tickets—I was washing a child, and said, "You are not in such a hurry"—she said "If you do not give me them this moment, I will chop your hands from your arms"—I did not mind her passion, because she had taken the toasting fork that morning, and beat her child all about the house because the cat broke the pie dish, and that was the game all day, till Mr. M'Gear, the tally-man, came, and then we were all ushered out of the room—I put down the account of the things I bought, and gave her child the paper, and said I had spent the money to the best advantage—she never asked where the things were got so long as she got them, and got the money to go into the country with that pot-boy.
ANN LYDIA LANK re-examined. Q. Is it so? A. It is quite false—I never gave her authority to pawn any goods—there is no truth in her statement—there were twenty-six duplicates found—I took her out of Charity.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.

2010. MARY ANN FOSTER was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of August, 1 pair of stays, value 2 s.; 2 basins, value 8 d.; 1 plate, value 4 d.; 1 yard of silk, value 1 s.; and 1 yard of patch-work, value 1 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Wright -2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of the ordinary of the Diocese.
ELIZABETH WRIGHT . I am a widow. I live at No. 6, Turville's-buildings, Turville-street, Bethnal-green—my husband died on the 2nd of August—the prisoner came to our house several times to fetch a few errands, as we were all laid up with a fever—we were all so bad we could not get out of bed—on the 2nd of August she came in and asked me if I would let her sit down in my room where I lodged—I left her in care of that room—my little girl is eleven years old—I was gone from half-past ten till eleven o'clock—this property was taken from my box—it was not locked—my husband was lying dead in the bed—I lost a pair of stays, two basins, a plate, a yard of silk, and some patch-work-when I returned the prisoner was gone, and the room was in darkness—I saw her again on the Thursday following, seven or eight days afterwards—these stays and basins are mine.
JOHN WILSON . I keep a chandler's shop. The prisoner brought these stays and basins to my shop, and asked if I would let her have a few provisions on them, to the amount of 6 1/2 d.—this was on the evening of the 2nd of August.
FREDERICK EAGER . I am a policeman. I went in search of the prisoner, and found her eight days afterwards, in Kingsland-road, in a state of great distress, and in a bad state of health—I know she has no place to
live in or to go to—she sleeps about in the brick-fields, or any where she can.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
